| 2026-07-18 | Bug Fix | NAS SNMP poll stalled the whole dashboard — cache-first, non-blocking reads (PR #153) | User reported "I just patched my desktop and the application is no longer coming up." The app was actually fine: after the reboot the tray auto-started with cold caches and the user opened it 90s in, while `/api/dashboard/summary` took **29.1s**. Live isolation of each collector found the culprit — `/api/storage/nas` at **25s**, while `/api/health` was 0.2s and `/api/storage/spaces` 8s. **Root cause**: `get_nas_storage()` polled each configured NAS **serially** over SNMP (~12s each, ~20 sequential round-trips per box) with **no cache**, sitting directly in the dashboard's collector fan-out — and the fan-out waits on its slowest member. Introduced in #148/#151; every other collector (events/BSOD/services/processes) already used a background cache worker, NAS didn't. **Fix** mirrors that existing pattern: cache-first **non-blocking** reads (dashboard gets the last snapshot instantly), **single-flight** background refresh thread (5m TTL), snapshot **persisted** to `nas_cache.json` (gitignored) so a restarted tray serves last-known state instead of blocking, **parallel** host polling, and a `pending` state that raises **no** concerns so a not-yet-polled NAS never renders as a false "NAS unreachable" warning. `/api/storage/nas` passes `wait=True` — blocks only on a first-ever visit. **Live verified on a cold tray with no cache file**: `/api/dashboard/summary` **29.1s → 0s**; warm NAS 0.12s → 0.01s → 0s; `nas_cache.json` written with both boxes reachable (shigs78nas 4 disks/2 vols, shigs78nas2 8 disks/4 vols); selftest **14/14 PASS**. **/code-review caught two of my own bugs, both fixed before merge**: `_save_nas_cache` promised "must never break a poll" but caught only `OSError` while `json.dump` raises `TypeError` — would have 500'd the Storage-tab route; and `wait=True` bypasses the single-flight guard so a tab visit could overlap the background refresh, two writers sharing one `.tmp` interleaving into a corrupt file (temp name now per-writer pid+tid). **Two unrelated pre-existing failures fixed to get a green gate** (both verified failing on clean `main` first): `test_snapshots` asserted an exact **live** drive count against a frozen fixture and broke the moment the E: Storage Spaces volume went offline (now asserts parsed-drive shape); and `test_architecture_html`'s `TEST_FILES` had silently fallen **4 files behind**, making the drift guard undercount by ~389 and cap the badge below the real test count — a drift guard that was itself drifting (added the files + a test keeping the list in sync with `tests/`). 3,046 tests, 87.07% coverage. **Prevention**: any collector added to the dashboard fan-out must be cached or background-refreshed. **Known issue, NOT from this change**: `dev.py verify`'s restart step now reproducibly fails (`/api/restart` kills the tray, re-exec never returns) — post-patch environment issue; verified manually instead. |
| 2026-07-26 | Feature | Name the add-in card by its detected PCIe switch chip on the Storage card view (PR #158 + fix #159) | Follow-up to #157: the user found the card is a SABRENT EC-P3X4, which uses the ASMedia ASM2812 switch I'd detected live — so the card view now names the card by its switch chip instead of the generic "PCIe M.2 carrier card". `disk.detect_pcie_switch(card_buses)` reads the card NVMe controllers' shared parent bridge via PnP (`DEVPKEY_Device_Parent`), maps VEN:DEV to a friendly name (ASM2812/2824, PLX PEX87xx, Marvell 88NR2241; else "<vendor> PCIe switch"). Route adds `card_switch`; frontend labels "PCIe M.2 carrier card · <switch>". **/code-review across #158+#159 caught 3 real issues, all fixed:** (a) [#158] onboard/AMD bridge could outvote the card switch — restricted to non-root-port vendors; (b) [#158] "PCIe switch switch" double-word in the label; (c) [#159, the big one] the #158 review fix filtered controllers by an "PCI Slot" LocationInfo string that **controllers never report** ("PCI bus N, device 0, function 0" is the controller's format; "PCI Slot N" is the disk's) → detection returned "" and the feature was a **no-op on real hardware**. Fixed by correlating on **PCIe bus**: PS returns per-controller {bus, ven_dev}, the route passes the card drives' buses, and only controllers on those buses are counted — onboard M.2 excluded by bus (any vendor), passive-bifurcation cards yield no switch name. **Live-verified on the deployed tray: card view now reads "PCIe M.2 carrier card · ASMedia ASM2812".** +~35 tests total, arch disk.py chip →1,716. **REUSABLE LESSONS: (1) an NVMe CONTROLLER's DEVPKEY_Device_LocationInfo is "PCI bus N, device D, function F" — the "PCI Slot N" string lives on the DISK's MSFT_PhysicalDisk.PhysicalLocation; don't cross them. (2) verify a detection feature against LIVE hardware, not just mocked tests — the #158 review fix passed all mocked tests but returned "" on the real box because the mock encoded the wrong assumption. (3) correlate card-vs-onboard by the drives' own PCIe buses, not by vendor guesses.** |
| 2026-07-26 | Feature | Physical PCIe card view on the Storage tab — pinpoint a failed drive for a swap (PR #157) | User replacing the failed Storage Spaces SSD wanted the instructions to show a physical view of the PCIe card to know exactly which drive failed. Diagnosed the topology live from the PCIe device tree (answering "can't you tell from the system?" — yes): the four 2TB pool drives sit on an aftermarket **ASMedia ASM2812 switch-based 4×M.2 card** (parent bridge `VEN_1B21&DEV_812B`, physical SLOT2); the failed **Samsung 990 PRO 2TB serial …20C4** is pool `Usage: Retired` (definitive dead-one marker even though SMART reads Healthy) on the switch's first downstream port. The two 1TB drives (990 PRO boot + SK hynix) are onboard M.2. **Delivered both:** (1) an immediate standalone HTML guide (SendUserFile) for the swap in progress; (2) in-app: `disk.build_pcie_card_topology(physical, members)` (pure) groups disks into add-in-card sockets (ordered by PCIe bus = switch-port order) vs onboard, correlating Get-PhysicalDisk with Storage Spaces Usage to flag the retired drive; route `GET /api/storage/pcie-topology`; frontend `dkLoadTopology()` draws an SVG card diagram (failed socket red) built with DOM APIs + textContent (no innerHTML). **/code-review (2-agent, accuracy lens) caught 4 real issues, all fixed:** (a) card membership gated on 'not integrated' → a SATA/USB/blank-location drive became a phantom socket + false has_card (now requires a real "PCI Slot"); (b) a drive fully dropped from Get-PhysicalDisk — the actual swap case — was flagged nowhere (now surfaced in a `missing` list + drives failed_serial); (c) a failed ONBOARD drive was hidden under "do not remove" (now marked red); (d) only last-4 serial shown though the point is "confirm by serial" (now full serial). **Live-verified in the browser on the deployed tray:** the card diagram shows Socket 1 = …20C4 ✖ RETIRED, sockets 2-4 healthy, onboard drives excluded. +18 tests, suite →3,086+, arch endpoints 146→147 + disk.py chip →1,581. **REUSABLE LESSONS: (1) a passive M.2 bifurcation card is invisible to software, but a SWITCH-based card exposes its switch chip (ASM2812) as the drives' shared PCIe parent — read the PnP parent bridge to group card drives + order by downstream port. (2) For a physical-swap aid, anchor identification on the drive SERIAL (foolproof) not slot position (Windows PCI-slot numbers ≠ card silk-screen). (3) Classify "on the card" by a positive PCI-Slot signal, never by "not integrated" — unknown-location/USB/SATA drives must not masquerade as card sockets.** Known: `dev.py verify`'s topology_basics check false-fails (homenet /api/homenet/topology takes ~12s live-scan vs the checker's 10s timeout) — pre-existing, unrelated, follow-up task_b0fc8e13. |
| 2026-07-26 | Feature | Elevated-kill fallback + anti-tamper classification for process "Access is denied" (PR #156) | User: "I should not be blocked from killing processes — e.g. Failed to kill mc-fw-host.exe: Access is denied." Diagnosed live: the tray runs **non-elevated** (startup task `-RunLevel Limited`, confirmed `IsAdmin False`), so "Access is denied" has **two causes needing different fixes**: (1) **elevation gap** — can't kill SYSTEM/other-user/higher-integrity procs (an elevated retry works); (2) **tamper protection** — security software (McAfee `mc-fw-host`, Defender PPL) is guarded by a kernel self-defence driver, so `TerminateProcess` is blocked even for admin (elevation can't help; must disable self-protection / uninstall). Confirmed mc-fw-host is case 2: even the **user-owned** instance has an unreadable exe path — the tell-tale of anti-tamper. **Fix:** `processes.classify_kill_failure(pid)` → `{reason: protected\|needs_admin\|gone, can_elevate, message}` (known security-product name OR same-user-but-unreadable-path ⇒ protected; cross-owner ⇒ needs_admin); `kill_process_elevated()` runs an elevated `System32\taskkill.exe /F /PID` via `_run_elevated` (ShellExecuteEx 'runas' UAC, isolated+mockable). `/api/processes/kill` enriches Access-denied responses with the classification; **new** `/api/processes/kill-elevated` (localhost-only, SAFE_PROCESSES guard re-applied). Frontend `proc_handleKillFailure`/`proc_elevatedKill`: offer "Kill as administrator" for elevatable cases, honest message for protected ones (both `killProc` + `killProcessFromConcern`). **/code-review (5-agent, security lens) caught a HIGH bug I introduced + I fixed it:** the elevated route's SAFE_PROCESSES guard **failed OPEN** — `except (AccessDenied, Exception): pass` fell through to elevated `taskkill /F` when the name was unreadable, i.e. exactly the SYSTEM procs (lsass/csrss/…) that would BSOD the box; now **fails closed** (403) with a regression test asserting the elevated kill isn't reached. Also hardened: absolute taskkill.exe path (no PATH-hijack), ctypes argtypes (no 64-bit HANDLE truncation). **Live-verified:** killing the real mc-fw-host.exe now returns `reason: protected, can_elevate: false` + the disable-self-protection message instead of bare "Access is denied"; process correctly stays running. +34 tests, suite →3,073+ , 87.13%. arch: +route chip, endpoints 145→146, processes.py chip →1,907. **REUSABLE LESSONS: (1) a non-elevated tray cannot kill SYSTEM/tamper-protected procs — classify the failure and be honest rather than showing a bare OS error. (2) When adding an ELEVATED variant of a guarded action, the guard MUST fail closed on the more-privileged path — an unreadable name is the exact case the guard protects, not a reason to skip it.** |
| 2026-07-26 | Bug Fix | /api/restart relaunched a pysnmp MIB file instead of the tray — argv-snapshot fix (PR #155) | Resolves the long-standing "restart kills the tray, replacement never starts" bug (prior chip task_920f39d9), which broke `dev.py verify`'s restart step. **Root cause, diagnosed live (not the prior session's theory):** pysnmp's MIB loader **rewrites `sys.argv[0]` at runtime** to a MIB source path (`...\pysnmp\smi\mibs\SNMPv2-SMI.py`) when the NAS SNMP collector runs. `_do_restart` spawned `[sys.executable, *sys.argv]`, so after any NAS poll the "restart" launched that MIB file instead of `tray.py` — the child exited instantly, leaving no replacement ("clean 202, then silence, no traceback"). PR #153's background NAS-refresh thread made the mutation automatic minutes after startup, which is why it "broke" ~2026-07-18 — **NOT** the Windows patch / Store `pythonw.exe` alias the prior session suspected (that theory was disproven: breakaway/job/silo/port-handoff harnesses all survived; only the real tray failed, and the parent trace showed argv = the MIB path). **Fix:** snapshot the launch command at import — before pysnmp can mutate it — into `_ORIGINAL_ARGV` (absolute, so CWD-independent) in both windesktopmgr.py and tray.py; relaunch `[sys.executable, *_ORIGINAL_ARGV]` with explicit `cwd=APP_DIR` in `_do_restart` (extracted to a testable module-level `_restart_worker`) and `tray.restart_app`; stay alive instead of `os._exit` if the spawn raises; add `restart.log` (gitignored) recording the exact relaunch cmd + child pid, closing the silent-failure blind spot. **Live-verified:** forced a NAS poll to mutate argv, then POST /api/restart relaunched `tray.py` (confirmed in restart.log) and the replacement served /api/health — where before it relaunched SNMPv2-SMI.py and died. **/code-review (5-agent) caught + I fixed:** a proposed 45→120s heartbeat bump was reverted (its rationale was factually wrong — `start_server` doesn't warm caches before Werkzeug binds; the argv fix alone makes the replacement answer within 45s), untested stay-alive branch (added regression tests), docstring drift, and tray-menu tracing parity. +10 tests, suite 3,046→3,054, 87.08%. arch test-count chip →3,054. **REUSABLE LESSON: never relaunch a process from live `sys.argv` — a shared library (pysnmp) can rewrite it at runtime; snapshot argv at import. And when a restart "silently dies," instrument the child's startup + the parent's exact spawn command before theorising about jobs/OS.** |
| 2026-09-05 | Feature | Elevated drive-swap script for Storage Spaces pool repair — web button deliberately dropped (PR #160) | User asked for "a script to do this and make it executable on the Storage tab" for the post-swap pool repair. Shipped `scripts/repair_storage_pool.ps1` (run manually, elevated): add new disk → `Repair-VirtualDisk` → poll jobs → **VERIFY space Healthy/OK** → only then `Remove-PhysicalDisk` the old entry. **The Storage-tab button was BUILT AND THEN REMOVED** after review: `POST /api/storage/pool-repair` was **CSRF-reachable** (`remote_addr` is a network check, not auth; and my `get_json(silent=True)` removed the 415 that incidentally guards the app's other localhost routes), so any site the user visited could trigger an elevated disk-removing run behind a bare "Windows PowerShell" UAC prompt — a permanent liability for a once-in-years operation. User chose script-only; panel + 3 routes + backend removed, so the app exposes nothing admin-privileged. **Three review rounds found real data-loss paths, all fixed:** verified only `vds[0]` while removal affects the whole pool (now refuses multi-space pools; repair/jobs scoped to the space); a `.`-suffixed serial normalised to `""` and could match a **healthy in-use** member (serial may now only NARROW the retired set and FAILS when unmatched); a `Repair-VirtualDisk` that threw looked identical to one that finished (now tracks a job was observed); substring `-match 'OK'` passed any status containing OK (now strict); a blank **USB stick** was `CanPool` and could be absorbed into the parity pool (BusType stringified — it can arrive as UInt16 — plus a size floor vs the smallest surviving member, since a missing disk reports Size 0); target re-resolved by UniqueId + re-asserted retired immediately before the irreversible command. **Two that would have bitten THIS machine:** the pool carries a **Suspended** repair job and the loop aborted on first sight of one (~20s after the add, before Windows resumes it) — now tolerated ~5 min; and it wasn't **re-runnable** (post-add failures left the disk pooled, so a re-run said "fit the replacement" for a drive already fitted) — now detects and resumes at the rebuild. Verified: PS parser clean, elevation guard refuses unelevated. Docs: run instructions in the script header, the user's swap guide, and [[project_storage_spaces_degraded]] (whose old procedure had the **removal before the repair** — corrected). **REUSABLE LESSONS: (1) `remote_addr == 127.0.0.1` is NOT authentication — a localhost route is reachable by any page the user visits, and non-silent `get_json()` is what incidentally blocks cross-site form posts, so never add `silent=True` to a destructive route. (2) Before automating a rare destructive op behind a button, weigh a permanent admin-privileged endpoint against a once-in-years convenience — a script the user runs is often the right answer. (3) A gate is only as good as its evidence: verify the rebuild actually RAN, not just that health reads OK afterwards.** |
| 2026-09-05 | Bug Fix | `dev.py verify` false-failed `topology_basics` on a healthy-but-slow topology fetch (PR #NN) | Resolves the known-issue noted in the #157/#153 rows (prior chip task_b0fc8e13). **Root cause (confirmed live):** `/api/homenet/topology` responds `ok=True` but takes ~12s because `build_topology()` does a live SNMP switch/Orbi scan on every request, while `scripts/post_deploy_topology_check.py`'s `_get()` used a flat 10s `urllib` timeout — so a healthy endpoint reported `topology fetch failed: timed out` and failed the gate. **Fix (minimal, task option a):** `_get()` gained a per-call `timeout` param (default `DEFAULT_TIMEOUT=10`); the five checks that hit `/api/homenet/topology` now pass `TOPOLOGY_TIMEOUT=30`. Fixed **all five** topology-hitting checks, not just `topology_basics` — the siblings (`moca_bridge_strict_vendors`, `no_self_referential_classifier_loop`, `orbi_satellite_visibility`, `no_phantom_blink_bridges`) `return None`→PASS on a fetch error, so at 10s they'd silently no-op into **false-passes** on the slow endpoint. +5 tests (timeout regression guards asserting every topology check requests the wider budget and that `_get` honors/defaults its timeout), suite →3,114, 87.31%. arch: post_deploy chip line-count refreshed ~320→~355 + timeout note. **/code-review flagged the deeper issue (task option b), deferred to a follow-up (task_0352c231): the checker fetches topology 5× and the endpoint isn't cached, so a healthy gate now runs ~5 serial ~12s live scans (~60s); the root-cause fix is to cache the endpoint like the NAS collector (#153) or fetch once and share. Kept scope to the minimal timeout fix per the task.** **REUSABLE LESSON: a post-deploy checker's timeout must budget for the endpoint's real live-work cost, not the fast/cached case — and a check that no-ops to PASS on fetch failure is a false-pass, so raise its timeout too, don't just fix the one check that happened to be `fail`-severity.** |
| 2026-09-06 | Bug Fix | A scan still running is not a failed scan (PR #165) | Live verification of the Cleanup tab (NOT tests — everything was green) caught it printing red "Scan timed out." while the backend worker was still running fine and about to cache its result. Two causes: the poll ceiling was ~3 min (120×1.5s) but a **cold** %TEMP% walk of 108k files took 3.5+ min after a tray restart — the earlier 56s figure had a warm FS cache; and giving up was rendered as a *failure*. Now ~12 min ceiling with backoff (20 polls at 1.5s then 3s), and still-running gets its own amber "it will finish on its own" message, distinct from a real error (red) and from no response. Also folded `mnt_scan` onto the shared `mnt_poll` helper — that third hand-rolled copy of the poll loop is exactly what let the wording drift. Playwright gate asserts the still-running text never says failed/timed out. **LESSON: a warm-cache measurement is not a budget. Timing something once, right after another run touched the same files, tells you almost nothing about the cold path a user actually hits after a restart.** |
| 2026-09-06 | Feature | Maintenance Tier 2 — space analysis: biggest files + duplicate finder (PR #164) | READ-ONLY analysis on the Cleanup tab. **Scoping matters most here: a codebase survey first showed HALF of "Tier 2" already existed on the Disk tab** — folder drill-down (`analyze_disk_path` + the "🔍 Analyze Space" modal) and the WinSxS component store (`_get_winsxs_actual_size` via DISM, in Quick wins). So only the two real gaps were built, and the Cleanup tab cross-links to the Disk tab for the rest instead of shipping a second copy. New: `scan_large_files()` (top-N biggest individual files, bounded min-heap so memory stays flat regardless of files walked) and `find_duplicates()` (size group → 64KB partial hash → full SHA-256; **size is never treated as proof**). Analysis-only by deliberate design — Tier 1 can delete because its categories are a whitelist of regenerated files, but the biggest file on a disk is as likely to be a VM image the user needs. Bounded by file/time/hash-byte ceilings with `truncated` surfaced in the UI. Refactor: #163's hardened concurrency became ONE generic registry `start_or_get()` rather than being hand-copied per scan (junk's `_scan_state`/`_scan_lock` survive as aliases). **Review caught 2 real issues**: `_scans` grew unboundedly because keys embedded raw (unclamped) `top_n`/`min_bytes` and un-normalised roots — `top_n=201/5000/999999` and `C:\X` vs `C:\X\` vs `C:/X` each minted a permanent entry on a tray that runs for weeks (now keyed off validated+clamped values, plus `_evict_stale()`); and the walk re-checked the clock only every 2000 files, so on a spun-down/network drive (~100ms per stat) one directory could overrun the 150s budget by minutes. A test I wrote also caught the file cap being enforced only *between* directories. **Verified live**: found a 282.6 MB zip in Downloads (walked 481 files), and on a purpose-built fixture found all 3 identical copies across nested folders (6.0 MB wasted) while correctly EXCLUDING a same-size-different-content decoy — proving hash confirmation, not size matching. Tiers 3 (SFC/DISM/TRIM) / 4 (registry read-only report) still planned. |
| 2026-09-06 | Feature | Maintenance Tier 1 — junk/temp Cleanup tab (preview-first) (PR #162, #163) | New 🧹 Cleanup tab + `maintenance.py` blueprint. Scan (read-only) previews 5 user-scope junk categories with count/bytes/samples — Temporary files (%TEMP%/%TMP%), Thumbnail & icon cache, Crash dumps, WER queue, Recycle Bin; `clean_junk(keys)` acts ONLY on whitelisted category KEYS (never caller paths), localhost-only, Python-first (os.scandir/remove/shutil.rmtree + ctypes SHFileOperation/EmptyRecycleBin). System-scope junk handed off to Windows' own Disk Cleanup (cleanmgr) so the app never runs an elevated file-deleter. **#162 review hardened 5 data-loss gaps** (reparse-point detection so junctions aren't traversed/deleted-through; min_age protects fresh files in an old dir via `_tree_has_recent`; every root confined to %USERPROFILE%; %TEMP%/%TMP% de-dup; Recycle Bin `default_off` since emptying is irreversible). **#163 made the scan non-blocking**: a %TEMP% walk of 108k files took 56s and blocked the request — now runs in a background daemon thread (single-flight via `_scan_lock`/`_scan_state`, ~90s cache), route returns `{status:running}` immediately and the frontend polls every 1.5s. #163 review hardened the concurrency: thread-start failure rolls back the running flag under the lock (else the tab wedged forever, force couldn't recover); failed scans no longer cached for the full TTL. **Verified live**: tab renders instantly with a scanning counter, background scan completes, 5 categories render with real sizes (1.8 GB reclaimable: temp 887.5 MB/108,669 files, thumbs 935.3 MB, dumps 19.3 MB, WER 0 B, bin 5.0 MB) and correct pre-check logic (checked iff bytes>0 and not default_off — WER & Recycle Bin correctly unchecked). Tiers 2 (space analysis), 3 (SFC/DISM/TRIM), 4 (registry read-only report) planned next. |
| 2026-09-10 | Feature | Maintenance Tier 4 - registry report: read-only, no fix button (PR #168) | Final tier of the 4 the user chose. **The honest answer mattered more than the feature: cleaning the registry does NOT make Windows faster**, and the report says so on screen. Measured BEFORE building, because a report that finds nothing is not worth shipping and one tuned to find something is dangerous: 209 uninstall entries -> 0 missing InstallLocations; 3,962 SharedDLLs -> 32 genuinely missing (a Dell update left refcounts - verified by hand, the folder holds 219 files and those 32 are truly absent); 26 startup entries -> 1 broken (a Run value of literally `""`). **Deliberately NOT reported: uninstall entries with no DisplayName** - 35 of 209 here are core Windows (AddressBook, Connection Manager, DirectDrawEx, DXM_Runtime, Fontcore). Flagging those is not finding junk, it is inventing it, and that is how this genre damages systems. Unresolvable targets are UNVERIFIED, never broken: host processes (rundll32/msiexec/cmd - the host always exists so its presence proves nothing), UNC paths, non-string value types, 32/64-bit siblings. No fix/clean/delete button exists and a playwright test asserts that absence; the only action is a .reg backup per key. **Three false-positive vectors caught by running it pre-review** (UNC existence-checking; unhandled 32/64-bit redirection; and my own test catching the sibling matcher skipping x86->native because `C:\Program Files` is a string PREFIX of `C:\Program Files (x86)` - now longest-prefix-first). **Review caught 2 more**: the UNC guard sat in `_host_guard` which only the STARTUP scanner reaches, so the other three still had the blind spot (a UNC InstallLocation on an offline share would read as broken); and architecture.html was un-updated (1,128->1,654 lines = 47%, Tier 4 still said 'planned', no test_maintenance.py chip existed at all). Review also flagged the 32-bit views were never scanned - adding WOW6432Node RunOnce/App Paths/SharedDLLs is what surfaced the 32 real Dell refcounts. Also fixed a test of mine with a REAL side effect: the export allowlist test called the live exporter, writing actual backup folders under %LOCALAPPDATA% and spawning reg.exe every run. **Verified live: 33 broken of 4,287 checked, one button on the panel ('Back up these keys').** **LESSON: for any 'find the problems' feature, measure the real hit rate BEFORE building - and when a category returns nothing, that is the honest answer, not a prompt to loosen the rule until it finds something.** |
| 2026-09-10 | Bug Fix | Remediation reported success for fixes that never ran (PR #167) | Found while building Tier 3, which established the tray runs UNELEVATED. `Clear Windows Update Cache` swallowed every per-item PermissionError then unconditionally returned `{ok: True, "Windows Update cache cleared."}` — unelevated it deleted NOTHING and said it worked. **Verified live after the fix: "Could not remove any of the 59 cached update file(s)"** — 59 real files it had never been touching while claiming success. Two causes: (1) counting exceptions cannot work because `shutil.rmtree(ignore_errors=True)` NEVER raises, so an undeletable dir looked identical to a deleted one — now the OUTCOME is checked (does the path still exist?) and removed/failed reported honestly; (2) `Repair Windows Image (DISM+SFC)` ran two commands for up to 30 min then said "finished with warnings — check Event Log", blaming the Event Log for a missing admin token — now checks elevation up front and refuses in a second, pointing at the Cleanup tab for the exact commands. Test quality: no existing test exercised the every-delete-denied path, and two `repair_image` tests asserting ok=False would have passed for the WRONG reason once the gate short-circuited, so all of them now state elevation explicitly. 7 regression tests added. **LESSON: a best-effort loop that swallows per-item errors must still report what it actually achieved — `ignore_errors=True` plus an unconditional success return is how an app ends up lying to its user.** |
| 2026-09-10 | Feature | Maintenance Tier 3 — system maintenance: read-only status + hand-offs (PR #166) | Third scoping win in a row: most of Tier 3 ALREADY existed — `remediation.py` has one-click DISM+SFC and Clear-WU-Cache — so those were not rebuilt, the Cleanup tab cross-links to that tab. Genuine gap = drive optimization/TRIM plus the fact that nothing reported whether maintenance was actually happening. New read-only panel (all verified readable unelevated): **SSD TRIM** via `fsutil` (NOT winreg — `DisableDeleteNotify` is ABSENT by default and absent means enabled, so the registry cannot tell "enabled" from "key missing"); **drive-optimization schedule** last-run/result/staleness via Task Scheduler COM (Python-first, no PS); **pending-reboot** via winreg where only CBS + WindowsUpdate are STRONG signals — `PendingFileRenameOperations` is set on plenty of healthy machines and would cry wolf, so it is context only. Repairs are hand-offs, never executed: `dfrgui` launches via disk.py's EXISTING tool allowlist (no new route), SFC/DISM shown as copyable commands. **Review caught a CRITICAL bug that would have shipped**: `_defrag_tasks()` lacked `pythoncom.CoInitialize()` — a Flask worker thread has no COM apartment so `Dispatch()` raises, the blanket except swallowed it, and the panel would report Unknown on EVERY real request while passing my main-thread hand check (reproduced: main=True, worker=False). Fixed with the baseline.py/thermals.py pattern; COM reads split into `_read_defrag_tasks` so every IUnknown is released before CoUninitialize. My own `test_live_call_does_not_raise` was complicit — it asserted only ok/keys so it passed with the feature dead; now asserts a real TRIM verdict, plus a thread-context invariant test and a live-tray playwright assertion (the only layer that catches this, since mocking Dispatch bypasses the apartment requirement). **Verified live: optimize.known=True on the real tray.** Endpoint synchronous at ~36ms measured. Tier 4 (registry read-only report) still planned. |
| 2026-09-11 | Bug Fix | Two `-n auto` test flakes fixed at the source, not with bigger sleeps (PR #169) | Both blocked commits twice in one session and passed in isolation, and they had DIFFERENT causes — only one was about time. **(1) baseline WMI enrichment — a real cache bug, not a missing reset.** `reset_globals` already called `_reset_wmi_enrich_cache()`. The *preceding* test deliberately times a worker out and ABANDONS it; that worker later wakes and its `finally` publishes into the module cache — under xdist CPU contention, scheduled AFTER the next test's reset, so the reset lost a race it could not see and the next test started with `{'NEW': {}}` in a cache it believed empty. Fixed with a **generation counter** bumped by every reset: a worker records its generation at start and refuses to publish into a newer one, making the reset authoritative rather than merely first (which is what its docstring already claimed). **(2) cloudcopy lifecycle — a poll loop that gave up quietly.** `for _ in range(100): sleep(0.05)` waited 5 s then FELL THROUGH into `assert state == 'finished'`, reporting `'running'` — the visible failure was a downstream symptom of a wait that simply ran out, and raising the count only moves the threshold. Replaced with an Event (`_session_idle`) set in the worker's finally AFTER the history row lands, so a waiter that sees idle is guaranteed to see the history; `wait_for_idle()` returns the instant the worker is done, so a 60 s ceiling costs nothing. **Review caught a third bug:** a failed `Thread.start()` left the session claimed forever (`_active_session_id` set, idle cleared, no worker to release either) — every later run refused with *"another session is active"* for the life of the process; `_start_worker_or_release()` now releases the claim, matching the guard baseline.py already had. Verified: 5 clean full `-n auto` runs + 6 back-to-back runs of the two files, and **both new guards proven by regression tests that FAIL without their fix** (reverting the generation check makes the abandoned worker repopulate the cache). 3,314 → 3,320 tests. **LESSON: an abandonable background worker must be FENCED against state it might outlive — a reset that only clears is a race, not a fence. And a poll loop that falls through on timeout reports the wrong failure: bound the wait on a condition and assert the wait itself.** |
| 2026-09-13 | Bug Fix | Firmware tab reported a BIOS update that did not exist (PR #170, #171, #172) | User spotted it from the version numbers: "BIOS update available: 0.2.24.0 (you have 2.24.0). Update immediately" on an up-to-date machine, while the SAME page also said "your BIOS is current, no update needed". **Root cause:** Windows Update packages Dell firmware as 0.MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH while the BIOS reports MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH, so WU's 0.2.24.0 IS the installed 2.24.0 - and the WU code path never compared at all, it did `result["update_available"] = True  # WU only shows pending updates`. That comment is false; WU keeps offering firmware already installed. Methods 1 (DCU) and 2 (catalog) compared correctly; only this one asserted. **Fixes:** `_ver_gt` lifted out of its closure and taught to align a single extra LEADING ZERO component (narrow on purpose - blanket zero-stripping would rank 0.9.1 above 1.0.0), with zero-padding so 2.24.0.0 == 2.24.0; the WU path now compares (and the OLD naive compare would have MISSED a real 0.2.25.0 update, so this fixed a false negative too); the Raptor Lake note stopped hardcoding "your BIOS is current" and derives it from the installed version vs _RAPTOR_MICROCODE_BIOS; a _BIOS_LOGIC_VERSION cache stamp discards verdicts written by old logic. **Same defect, 2nd instance, also fixed:** `windowsdrivermgr.version_newer` ranked 31.0.15.5222 vs 31.0.15.5222.0 (same driver) as an update because a longer list wins on length alone. **#171 - the first fix did NOT work live.** Verified on the tray and it still said update available: logic_version was 3 (new code running) but current_version was EMPTY, and `_ver_gt` fell back to `latest != current`, so an unreadable BIOS version produced a phantom update - the exact bug class, sitting in my own fallback. Now fails safe (no parseable version on either side -> False), the parseability check moved BEFORE _align_versions (padding turned an empty side into [0,0,..] and defeated the guard), and an uncomputable verdict is no longer cached. **Review also caught my own tests doing real network I/O** - they skipped DCU but not urllib, so they downloaded Dell's CatalogPC.cab and ran expand.exe; the catalog IS reachable here (0.4s) so they passed by luck at 1-2s each. **summarize_bios and version_newer had ZERO tests**, which is how a self-contradicting page shipped; 35 added. **LESSON: in any 'is there an update / is this broken' check, the FALLBACK must fail safe. `different != newer`. And re-verify LIVE after the fix - the first one looked right in every test and still misbehaved on the real machine.** |
| 2026-09-27 | Feature | Documentation tab with the SSD replacement procedure (PR #173) | User asked where the tab showing the drive-swap steps was - there wasn't one. The steps lived in a script header, a memory note, and an HTML file sent in chat weeks earlier that no longer existed. New 📖 Docs tab (prefix `doc`) puts the procedure in the app next to the hardware. **Live data, not a screenshot**: reuses the Storage tab's card diagram via `dkLoadTopology(sectionId, hostId, prefetched)` and reads the retired flag/socket/serial from the running system - a swap guide whose 'which drive failed' is a picture is wrong the moment you act on it. Text cross-checked line-by-line against `scripts/repair_storage_pool.ps1`, including the ordering trap (never Remove-PhysicalDisk before the rebuild verifies; PhysicalDiskRedundancy=1 can lose the volume) and why parity cannot self-heal (rebuilds into FREE space; the only free space was on the dead drive). **Three defects found while building**: (1) the diagram never appeared - `/api/storage/pcie-topology` took 30-51s (3 PowerShell reads), now the two independent reads overlap (~13s cold) + 10min cache + single-flight compute lock; the real killer was the page fetching it TWICE on open, and browsers cap connections per host so the second queued behind dashboard polling - one shared fetch now renders in ~500ms warm; (2) the placeholder claimed 'No add-in card detected' WHILE still loading, a factual claim about hardware shown to someone standing over an open case; (3) PDF export came out BLANK because it printed the app page via @media print and the shell's layout (fixed rail, scroll container, per-tab display toggling) fought it - now clones into an isolated same-origin iframe with its own stylesheet, built via createElement/textContent so live drive strings cannot inject markup. **Review caught 2 more**: the export could capture the loading placeholder (same bug class as #2, unfixed in the export path - now awaits the live reads; verified by exporting mid-load); and the tab loaded once per session with no refresh, so the documented workflow (open tab -> shut down -> swap -> boot -> come back) would still flag the REPLACED drive as 'REMOVE THIS ONE' - now reloads every visit + explicit Refresh, matching baseline/backup/utilities. **Verified live on deployed code**: diagram renders, export is 5,453 chars with the real serial 20C4, no placeholders, titled 'SSD replacement procedure'. **LESSONS: (a) `window.print()` blocks hard under automation - it hung a test browser 30 min; stub it deterministically rather than racing the deferred timer. (b) When a page makes a freshness promise in its own copy, the one-shot `_tabLoaded` gate silently breaks it. (c) A fix applied to the on-screen render does not automatically apply to the export of that render.** |

| 2026-10-10 | Bug Fix | Baseline timeline stops re-offering changes the baseline already holds (PR #189) | User: after "Accept current as baseline" the change timeline still asked to accept every old cluster (63 "Accept all N" buttons, live drift 0). Root cause: PR #99's acceptance watermark only gated the DASHBOARD; the timeline never compared events with the accepted baseline, so per-cluster accepts never cleared a cluster either. Fix: `baseline.annotate_reconciled()` marks an event reconciled when recorded at/before the last full accept or when the baseline reflects it (added->key present, removed->absent, changed->new values held); clusters show "In baseline" and partly-accepted ones offer/send only what is left.
